The [X-38][ref_x38] was built to bring seven people down from the [International Space Station][ref_iss] in
an emergency, and it flew the [X-24A][related_post_a321_martin_marietta_x24] shape to do it.
**Then it threw the runway away.** This is the thirty-ninth article in the
[X-Planes series][related_post_a297_xplanes_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the
[X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the [X-3][related_post_a300_douglas_x3], the
[X-4][related_post_a301_northrop_x4], the [X-5][related_post_a302_bell_x5], the
[X-6][related_post_a303_convair_x6], the [X-7][related_post_a304_lockheed_x7], the
[X-8][related_post_a305_aerojet_x8], the [X-9][related_post_a306_bell_x9], the
[X-10][related_post_a307_north_american_x10], the [X-11][related_post_a308_convair_x11], the
[X-12][related_post_a309_convair_x12], the [X-13][related_post_a310_ryan_x13], the
[X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14], the [X-15][related_post_a312_north_american_x15], the
[X-16][related_post_a313_bell_x16], the [X-17][related_post_a314_lockheed_x17], the
[X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18], the [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19], the
[X-20][related_post_a317_boeing_x20], the [X-21][related_post_a318_northrop_x21], the
[X-22][related_post_a319_bell_x22], the [X-23][related_post_a320_martin_marietta_x23], the
[X-24][related_post_a321_martin_marietta_x24], the [X-25][related_post_a322_bensen_x25], the
[X-26][related_post_a323_schweizer_x26], the [X-27][related_post_a324_lockheed_x27], the
[X-28][related_post_a325_osprey_x28], the [X-29][related_post_a326_grumman_x29], the
[X-30][related_post_a327_rockwell_x30], the [X-31][related_post_a328_rockwell_mbb_x31], the
[X-32][related_post_a329_boeing_x32], the [X-33][related_post_a330_lockheed_martin_x33], the
[X-34][related_post_a331_orbital_sciences_x34], the [X-35][related_post_a332_lockheed_martin_x35], the
[X-36][related_post_a333_mcdonnell_douglas_x36], and the [X-37][related_post_a334_boeing_x37].

**Five vehicles earlier in this series led up to the horizontal landing and this one was built to abandon it.**
The [X-23][related_post_a320_martin_marietta_x23] proved a lifting entry could be steered to a point. The
[X-24][related_post_a321_martin_marietta_x24] spent its entire life characterising the unpowered approach.
The [X-33][related_post_a330_lockheed_martin_x33] and [X-34][related_post_a331_orbital_sciences_x34] were
sized around runway operations, and the [X-37][related_post_a334_boeing_x37] made an autonomous runway
landing routine.
**The X-38 flies the same [lifting body][ref_lifting_body] family of shape through entry and then comes down under seven hundred square metres of ram-air canopy onto whatever flat ground is available.**
The [space agency's own account][ref_nasa_x38] of the programme describes it in exactly those terms.

**That is not a smaller ambition. It is a different answer to a question the series has been asking since the X-23**,
and the question is what a runway costs.

## The Research Question

### Why a Station Needs a Lifeboat at All

A crew aboard a space station cannot walk home. If somebody suffers a medical emergency, if the station is
damaged, or if the vehicle that brought them is unavailable, the only way down is a vehicle already docked
and waiting. **The requirement is therefore not performance but availability**, and a vehicle that spends
years attached to a station doing nothing is judged by what it costs to have rather than by what it does.

**Seven seats is the number that makes the requirement bite.** The station's design complement was seven,
the Soyuz that served as the interim lifeboat carried three \[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\]
\[[Soyuz spacecraft][ref_soyuz]\], and a station cannot hold more people than it can evacuate. **The crew size of the station and the seat count of its lifeboat are the same number**, which
is why this vehicle exists at all and why it is as large as it is.

### The Question This Article Asks

**What does a runway landing cost a returning vehicle, and what does giving it up buy?** Both sides are
computable from published numbers, and the answer turns out to be a single ratio that this article spends
its length unpacking.

### Why the Runway Is the Expensive Choice

A runway landing demands four things at once. **It demands speed**, because a lifting body must fly fast
enough to generate lift on a small wing. **It demands a runway**, which is a prepared strip several
kilometres long that somebody must own and maintain. **It demands crossrange**, because there are very few
such strips and the vehicle must be able to reach one from wherever the orbit happens to put it. And
**it demands that the vehicle fly**, with the control authority, the landing gear and the handling qualities
that implies.

**A parafoil demands none of the four.** It touches down at walking pace, it needs a field rather than a
strip, it needs almost no crossrange because fields are everywhere, and it does not need the vehicle to fly
at all once the canopy is out.

## Programme Origin

### An Interim Answer That Would Not Scale

The station's early operations relied on a docked [Soyuz][ref_soyuz] as the escape vehicle, and a Soyuz
seats three.
**A permanently crewed station of seven therefore needed either three Soyuz vehicles or one purpose-built lifeboat**,
and the second looked cheaper.

The programme began at the [Johnson Space Center][ref_jsc] in early 1995 as a technology demonstration for a
Crew Return Vehicle. It was deliberately structured as a fast and cheap effort, using commercial hardware where
it could and an existing aerodynamic shape rather than a new one \[[NASA account of the X-38 prototype crew return vehicle][ref_nasa_x38]\].

### Why the Shape Was Borrowed

**The X-24A shape was chosen because its aerodynamics were already known**, having been flown twenty-eight
times between 1969 and 1971 \[[Martin Marietta X-24A][ref_x24a]\] \[[NASA account of the X-38 prototype crew return vehicle][ref_nasa_x38]\]. That is an unusually direct inheritance for an X-plane and it saved the
programme the wind-tunnel campaign a new lifting body would have needed.

### Who Built the Airframes

[Scaled Composites][ref_scaled] built the atmospheric test airframes \[[NASA account of the X-38 prototype crew return vehicle][ref_nasa_x38]\], which is why the designation
carries that name, while the space agency integrated the systems and the [European Space Agency][ref_esa] and the
[German Aerospace Center][ref_dlr] took part in the programme \[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\].
**International participation in an X-plane is unusual and it is a consequence of the station being international**,
since the vehicle existed to serve a station that Europe helped build.

### The Window This Article Calls the Period

**The programme ran from 1995 to its cancellation on 29 April 2002**, and this article calls that window
plus a few years of settling the period, taking the cutoff at 2006. Everything after is contemporary.
**The reference counts reported later use exactly that boundary.**

## Sizing From First Principles

### Notation

**Parachute engineering and flight mechanics each own a set of symbols and the two overlap.** The ambiguous
cases are marked here rather than resolved silently at each use.

| Symbol | Meaning | The other standard use, avoided here |
|---|---|---|
| $S$ | canopy area | the wing reference area, which does not appear. The drogue is $S_{d}$ |
| $\delta$ | glide angle below the horizontal | |
| $C_{R}$ | resultant force coefficient | |
| $n$ | scaling exponent | the crew count, written $c$, and the load factor, written in $g$ throughout |
| $\Lambda$ | opening-shock factor | |
| $V$ | a speed under the canopy | $v$, which is every other speed, including the runway and orbital ones |
| $a$ | semi-major axis | acceleration, which always carries a bar or a subscript |
| $h$ | an altitude | $H$, which is the atmospheric scale height |
| $e$ | the base of the natural logarithm | eccentricity, which does not appear |
| $L/D$ | **a ratio used for two different aerodynamics.** The canopy glides at 3 and the vehicle enters at about 0.8, and every use says which | |

### What the Landing System Has to Absorb

**This is the whole argument in one number and everything after it is elaboration.**

A lifting body arriving on a runway touches down at something near ninety metres per second. The kinetic
energy that the landing system, the brakes and the runway between them must dissipate is

$$E = \tfrac{1}{2} m v^{2}$$

which for the 11,340 kilogram vehicle at that speed is **45.93 megajoules**.

The same mass under a ram-air canopy arrives very differently. A gliding canopy in steady descent balances
weight against the **resultant** aerodynamic force rather than against lift alone. A
[ram-air canopy][ref_parafoil] is a wing rather than a drag device, so the airspeed follows from a resultant
coefficient,

$$C_{R} = C_{L} \sqrt{1 + \frac{1}{(L/D)^{2}}}, \qquad V = \sqrt{\frac{2 W}{\rho S C_{R}}}$$

and the two components follow from the glide angle, which is fixed by the glide ratio alone,

$$\delta = \arctan \frac{1}{L/D},$$

$$V_{\text{vertical}} = V \sin \delta = \frac{V}{\sqrt{1 + (L/D)^{2}}}, \qquad V_{\text{horizontal}} = V \cos \delta = (L/D) \, V_{\text{vertical}}.$$

At a glide ratio of three that angle is **18.43 degrees** below the horizontal.

- [Heinrich et al 1964][research_heinrich_nietz_1964]
- [Goodrick 1969][research_goodrick_1969]
- [Nicolaides and Tragarz 1971][research_nicolaides_tragarz_1971]
- [Nicolaides 1971][research_nicolaides_1971]

Taking a lift coefficient of 0.80 and a glide ratio of 3, which are ordinary for a large ram-air canopy, the
11,340 kilogram vehicle under **696.8 square metres** of canopy descends at **17.58 metres per second** of
airspeed, being **16.68 forward and 5.56 down**. Its kinetic energy is **1.752 megajoules**.

**The ratio is 26.2 to one**, and the mass cancels out of it entirely, so the ratio is the square of the
speed ratio and depends on no property of the vehicle at all.

$$\frac{E_{\text{runway}}}{E_{\text{parafoil}}} = \left( \frac{v_{\text{runway}}}{V} \right)^{2} = 26.2$$

**That is what the runway costs.** A vehicle that lands horizontally arrives carrying twenty-six times the
energy of one that lands under a canopy, and every part of the airframe that touches the ground has to be
built for it.

- [Ray et al][research_ericsray_leonarddcassady]
- [Ray and Janssen][research_ericsray_samuelallenjanssen]
- [Morris and Hill][research_aaronlmorris_jenniferrhill]
- [Batemen 1920][research_batemenh_1920]
- [Sullivan 1944][research_sullivan_1944]
- [Sherer 1945][research_sherer_jr_1945]

### How Much of That Rests on the Assumed Lift Coefficient

**The lift coefficient of 0.80 is the least defensible input in this article and it deserves its own sensitivity.**
It appears once, under a radical, so every speed varies as

$$V \propto C_{L}^{-1/2}$$

and the parafoil's kinetic energy therefore as $C_{L}^{-1}$, which makes the energy ratio vary as the
**first** power,

$$\frac{E_{\text{runway}}}{E_{\text{parafoil}}} \propto C_{L}.$$

| Lift coefficient | Sink rate | Against 0.80 | Energy ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.60 | 6.42 metres per second | plus 15.5 percent | 19.7 |
| 0.80 | 5.56 metres per second | reference | 26.2 |
| 1.00 | 4.97 metres per second | minus 10.6 percent | 32.8 |

**The individual sink rates move and the conclusion does not.** A factor of 1.67 across the plausible range
of the coefficient moves the ratio between 19.7 and 32.8, and the argument needs only that the ratio is
large.

- [Wei and Pearson 1974][research_wei_pearson_1974]
- [Nicolaides 1976][research_nicolaides_1976]
- [Pearson et al 1977][research_pearson_wei_1977]
- [Matsuo and Butler 1983][research_matsuo_butler_1983]

### The Flare, and an Identity That Needs No Assumption

A parafoil arrests its descent at the last moment by pulling both control lines, raising the angle of attack
and converting forward motion into a transient increase in lift.
**The question is whether there is enough forward motion to do it with**, and the answer needs nothing
assumed.

In steady glide the horizontal speed is the vertical multiplied by the glide ratio, so the two kinetic
energies stand in the ratio

$$\frac{E_{\text{horizontal}}}{E_{\text{vertical}}} = \frac{\tfrac{1}{2} m V_{\text{horizontal}}^{2}}{\tfrac{1}{2} m V_{\text{vertical}}^{2}} = (L/D)^{2}$$

**exactly, with no mass, no area and no air density appearing.** At a glide ratio of three the forward
motion carries **9.0 times** the energy of the descent.

| Glide ratio | Forward energy against vertical |
|---|---|
| 2.0 | 4.0 times |
| 3.0 | 9.0 times |
| 4.0 | 16.0 times |

**The flare is therefore never limited by energy.** It is limited by how quickly the canopy can convert that
energy into lift before it stalls, which is a question about the canopy and not about the arithmetic.
Converting only five percent of what is available takes the sink rate from 5.56 to
**4.12 metres per second**. If the canopy turns a fraction $\eta$ of the forward energy into arrested
descent, then since the forward energy is $(L/D)^{2}$ times the vertical, the vertical energy falls to
$1 - \eta (L/D)^{2}$ of its value, and with the unrounded sink rate of 5.559 metres per second

$$V_{\text{vertical}}' = V_{\text{vertical}} \sqrt{1 - \eta \, (L/D)^{2}} = 5.559 \sqrt{1 - 0.05 \times 9} = 4.12 \text{ metres per second}.$$

- [Meyn and Bennett 1991][research_meynlarrya_bennettmarks_1991]
- [Further development and flight test of an autonomous...][research_murrayjamese_simalexg_1994]
- [Losilevskii 1995][research_losilevskii_1995]
- [Tezduyar et al 1997][research_tezduyar_kalro_1997]

### What a Touchdown Costs the Occupants

A vertical arrival arrested over a stroke $s$ imposes a mean deceleration

$$\bar{a} = \frac{V_{\text{vertical}}^{2}}{2 s}.$$

| Arresting stroke | Deceleration at 5.56 metres per second |
|---|---|
| 15 centimetres | 10.50 g |
| 30 centimetres | 5.25 g |
| 60 centimetres | 2.63 g |
| 100 centimetres | 1.58 g |

After a flare to 4.12 metres per second the same thirty centimetres gives **2.89 g**,

$$\frac{\bar{a}}{g_{0}} = \frac{(4.122 \text{ m/s})^{2}}{2 \times 0.30 \text{ m} \times 9.80665 \text{ m/s}^{2}} = 2.89,$$

where $g_{0}$ is standard gravity and 4.122 metres per second is the unrounded flared sink rate.

**The runway case is not this calculation and the difference matters.** A runway landing is a rollout over
hundreds of metres rather than an impact, which is exactly why it needs a runway. Arresting ninety metres
per second in thirty centimetres would be **1,376.6 g**, which is not a landing but a crash,

$$\frac{\bar{a}}{g_{0}} = \frac{(90 \text{ m/s})^{2}}{2 \times 0.30 \text{ m} \times 9.80665 \text{ m/s}^{2}} = 1{,}376.6.$$

**The parafoil converts a distance problem into a stroke problem**, and a stroke of half a metre is
something a seat and a crushable structure can provide while a runway is something a nation must build.

- [Mennu et al][research_matlockmennu_jacobputnam]
- [Mazelsky et al 1963][research_mazelsky_platus_1963]
- [Black 1964][research_black_1964]
- [Walton et al 1964][research_walton_here_1964]
- [Hackenberger and Mattenson 1965][research_hackenberger_mattenson_1965]
- [Landing Characteristics of the Apollo Spacecraft with...][research_stubbssandym_1965]
- [Cloutier 1966][research_cloutier_1966_b]

### Why the Canopy Had to Be the Largest Ever Flown

The canopy carries a [wing loading][ref_wing_loading] of **159.6 newtons per square metre**, which is
**16.27 kilograms per square metre** or **3.33 pounds per square foot**,

$$\frac{W}{S} = \frac{11{,}340 \times 9.80665}{696.8} = 159.6 \ \text{N/m}^{2}, \qquad \frac{m}{S} = \frac{11{,}340}{696.8} = 16.27 \ \text{kg/m}^{2}, \qquad \frac{25{,}000 \ \text{lb}}{7{,}500 \ \text{ft}^{2}} = 3.33 \ \text{lb/ft}^{2}.$$

Most student skydivers start between 0.7 and 0.9 pounds per square foot, and experienced jumpers fly between
1.1 and 1.8 \[[Wingload Calculator, Skydive Perris][ref_perris_wingload]\]. So this canopy is loaded
**3.7 to 4.8 times** as heavily as a student's and **1.9 to 3.0 times** as heavily as an experienced
jumper's,

$$\frac{3.333}{0.9} = 3.70, \quad \frac{3.333}{0.7} = 4.76, \qquad \frac{3.333}{1.8} = 1.85, \quad \frac{3.333}{1.1} = 3.03.$$

**The area follows from the sink rate the designer will accept, and it follows steeply.** Inverting the
glide relation for area,

$$S = \frac{2 W}{\rho \, C_{R} \, V^{2}} \qquad\text{with}\qquad V = V_{\text{vertical}} \sqrt{1 + (L/D)^{2}}$$

so the area needed varies as the inverse square of the sink rate.

| Sink rate demanded | Canopy area | Against the flown canopy |
|---|---|---|
| 3.0 metres per second | 2,392.3 square metres | 343.3 percent |
| 4.0 metres per second | 1,345.7 square metres | 193.1 percent |
| 5.0 metres per second | 861.2 square metres | 123.6 percent |
| 6.0 metres per second | 598.1 square metres | 85.8 percent |

**Wanting one metre per second less costs half as much canopy again.** That is the relation that made this
the largest parafoil ever made \[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\], and it means the record was an output of the requirement rather than an
ambition of the programme.

- [Potvin 1997][research_potvin_1997]
- [Lee and Buckley 2000][research_lee_buckley_2000]
- [Potvin et al 2001][research_potvin_peek_2001]
- [Gimadieva 2001][research_gimadieva_2001]
- [Barrows 2002][research_barrows_2002]

### But the Crew Count Sets the Mass Only at the Margin

The obvious next step is to say that seven seats made the vehicle heavy and the heavy vehicle made the
canopy large. **That is only partly true and the arithmetic says by how much.**

**The model is stated because it is doing real work.** Taking a fraction $\phi$ of the empty mass as
independent of the crew, being the airframe, the heat shield, the deorbit stage and the avionics, and
scaling the remainder with the seat count $c$,

$$m(c) = m_{\text{empty}} \left( \phi + (1 - \phi) \frac{c}{c_{\max}} \right) + m_{\text{crew}} \, c$$

with $\phi$ taken as 0.55 and $m_{\text{crew}}$ as 97.3 kilograms. Anchoring on the sink rate the flown
canopy actually gives, so that the seven-crew row reproduces the flown area by construction,

| Crew | Approximate mass | Canopy area | Against the flown canopy |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 6,645 kilograms | 408.3 square metres | 58.6 percent |
| 3 | 8,210 kilograms | 504.5 square metres | 72.4 percent |
| 5 | 9,775 kilograms | 600.6 square metres | 86.2 percent |
| 7 | 11,340 kilograms | 696.8 square metres | 100.0 percent |

**A single-seat version would still have needed 58.6 percent of the canopy**, because the airframe, the heat
shield, the avionics and the deorbit stage do not shrink with the crew. At a fixed sink rate the area
relation makes area proportional to mass, so

$$\frac{S(1)}{S(7)} = \frac{m(1)}{m(7)} = \frac{6{,}645}{11{,}340} = 0.586.$$

**Six of the seven seats cost about forty percent of the canopy area**, and the fixed overhead costs the
rest.

**The fixed fraction is assumed and the conclusion survives the whole plausible range of it.**

| Fixed fraction | Single-seat mass | Canopy needed |
|---|---|---|
| 0.40 | 5,275 kilograms | 46.5 percent |
| 0.55 | 6,645 kilograms | 58.6 percent |
| 0.70 | 8,015 kilograms | 70.7 percent |

**Even at the most generous assumption a single-seat vehicle needs nearly half the canopy**, so the
statement that the crew count sets the mass only at the margin does not depend on the value chosen.

- [Goglia 1952][research_goglia_1952]
- [Braun 1953][research_braun_1953]
- [Phipps 1954][research_phipps_1954]

- [Miller 1954][research_miller_1954]
- [Shepardson 1954][research_shepardson_1954]
- [Lavier 1955][research_lavier_1955]
- [Lavier 1955][research_lavier_1955_b]
- [Goglia et al 1955][research_goglia_lavier_1955]
- [Sweeney 1955][research_sweeney_1955]
- [Smollett 1955][research_smollett_1955]
- [Lavrakas 1955][research_lavrakas_1955]
- [Lavrakas and Katz 1955][research_lavrakas_katz_1955]

### Why It Deploys in Five Stages, and Why the Obvious Model Fails

The flown system inflated the canopy in five [reefed][ref_reefing] stages \[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\].
**The obvious explanation is that a single-step inflation would injure the crew, and that explanation does not survive being written down.**

**Where that deployment speed comes from is itself worth asking**, because the vehicle is released at
several hundred kilometres per hour and a ram-air canopy cannot be opened anywhere near that. A drogue
brings it down first, and the area needed follows from the steady descent condition,

$$S_{d} = \frac{2 W}{\rho \, C_{D} \, V_{\text{target}}^{2}}.$$

| Target speed | Drogue area at a drag coefficient of 0.55 |
|---|---|
| 24 metres per second | 573.1 square metres |
| 27 metres per second | 452.8 square metres |
| 32 metres per second | 322.4 square metres |

**The drogue is itself a very large parachute**, at 452.8 square metres or **65.0 percent** of the main
canopy,

$$\frac{S_{d}}{S} = \frac{452.8}{696.8} = 0.650.$$

**The descent system is therefore two large decelerators in sequence rather than one**, and the
second cannot be opened until the first has done its work.

- [Fluid-Structure Interaction Simulations Of Supersonic...][research_francoiscadieux_michaelfbarad]
- [French 1963][research_french_1963]
- [French 1964][research_french_1964]
- [Melzig and Schmidt 1965][research_melzig_schmidt_1965]
- [Figucia and McCafferty 1966][research_figucia_mccafferty_1966]

At a deployment speed near twenty-seven metres per second the dynamic pressure is

$$q = \tfrac{1}{2} \rho V^{2} = 446.5 \text{ pascals}$$

and a fully inflated canopy of 696.8 square metres at a drag coefficient of 0.75 would pull

$$F = q \, S \, C_{D} = 233.3 \text{ kilonewtons},$$

which on 11,340 kilograms is **2.10 g** steady. Inverting for the area a deceleration limit admits,

$$S_{\text{admissible}} = \frac{m \, g_{\text{limit}} \, g_{0}}{q \, C_{D}}$$

gives **996.2 square metres** at three g, which is **1.43 times the whole canopy.**

**The model says one stage and the flown system used five.**

**Inverting for the speed at which the steady load would bite is more informative than the area**, and it
attaches a margin to the claim instead of leaving the reader to assume one is large,

$$V_{\text{limit}} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \, m \, g_{\text{limit}} \, g_{0}}{\rho \, S \, C_{D}}}.$$

| Deceleration limit | Speed at which the full canopy reaches it | Against the deployment speed |
|---|---|---|
| 2 g | 26.36 metres per second | 0.98 times |
| 3 g | 32.28 metres per second | 1.20 times |
| 5 g | 41.68 metres per second | 1.54 times |

**The margin is only about twenty percent in speed at a three g limit, and at a two g limit there is no margin at all.**
That qualification matters, so the article states it and does not rest on the three g figure alone.

- [Heinrich and Jamison 1966][research_heinrich_jamison_1966]
- [French 1969][research_french_1969]
- [French 1969][research_french_1969_b]
- [Preisser and Greene 1970][research_preisser_greene_1970]
- [Heinrich and Hektner 1970][research_heinrich_hektner_1970]
- [Huckins 1971][research_huckins_1971]

Adding an opening-shock factor does not close the gap either, since even at the high end of the usual range,

$$F_{\text{peak}} = \Lambda \, q \, S \, C_{D}, \qquad \Lambda \in [1.5, 2.5]$$

the peak reaches **5.25 g**, which a seated and restrained crew survives.

**What the failed calculation locates is that the constraint is not the crew.** A canopy of this size cannot
inflate uniformly. The centre cells fill first, the load concentrates on a fraction of the suspension lines,
and the limiting structure is the canopy itself rather than the occupants.
**Reefing exists to make the inflation orderly rather than to make it gentle**, and no vehicle-level load
model can predict the stage count because the stage count is not a vehicle-level quantity.

**A forward calculation that fails by this margin is a finding about the model and not a reason to discard it.**
It says precisely which physics the simple treatment omits.

- [Talay and Poole 1971][research_talaytheodorea_poolelamontr_1971]
- [Poole 1973][research_poole_1973]

- [Wolf 1974][research_wolf_1974]
- [Roberts 1974][research_roberts_1974]
- [McVey and Wolf 1974][research_mcvey_wolf_1974]
- [Bacchus et al 1975][research_bacchusdl_vickersjr_1975]
- [Heinrich and Saari 1978][research_heinrich_saari_1978_b]
- [Heinrich and Saari 1978][research_heinrich_saari_1978]
- [Roberts 1980][research_roberts_1980]
- [Garrard and Konicke 1981][research_garrard_konicke_1981]
- [Wolf and Croll 1981][research_wolf_croll_1981]
- [Purvis 1982][research_purvis_1982]
- [Aume et al 1983][research_aume_mcdaniel_1983]

### The Crossrange Argument Turned Inside Out

The lifting-entry vehicles earlier in this series, among them the [X-23][related_post_a320_martin_marietta_x23]
and the [X-37][related_post_a334_boeing_x37], treat crossrange as a figure of merit.
**The X-38 shows that it was never a figure of merit. It was the price of insisting on a runway.**

A parafoil steers only within its own glide cone from the point of deployment. The reach is height traded
for distance at the glide ratio,

$$R = \left( \frac{L}{D} \right) h,$$

which carries no mass, no area and no air density, and at a glide ratio of three from six kilometres it is
**18.0 kilometres**. The [X-37][related_post_a334_boeing_x37] article computed **1,397.8 kilometres** of
entry crossrange for a runway lander at a hypersonic lift-to-drag ratio near 1.1.
**The ratio is 77.7 to one and it looks devastating until the question is asked the other way round.**

**Crossrange exists to reach a runway, and runways are rare.** A vehicle that may land on any flat ground
needs the footprint only to choose between nearby fields, and the deorbit burn does the rest of the work by
choosing when to leave orbit.

| Landing sites available | Number | Reachable per day at an 18 kilometre footprint |
|---|---|---|
| Runways certified for a lifting body | 3 | 0.1 |
| Airfields of adequate length | 200 | 4.0 |
| Flat unobstructed areas | 20,000 | 396.1 |

**The last row is the answer.** A vehicle that needs a certified runway gets an opportunity every ten days.
A vehicle that needs a flat field gets four hundred a day.
**That is a factor of several thousand in availability, bought by giving up a factor of seventy-eight in crossrange**,
and for a lifeboat availability is the entire requirement.

**These are order-of-magnitude figures and the article claims nothing finer.** The site counts are
illustrative and the reachability model treats sites as uniformly distributed.
**What survives the crudeness is the direction and the scale of the trade**, which is all the argument
needs.

## Dependent Systems

### The Entry, Which Is the Part Nobody Tested

The vehicle deorbits from a station orbit near four hundred kilometres
\[[International Space Station][ref_iss]\]. Lowering perigee far enough to
guarantee entry costs

$$v = \sqrt{\mu \left( \frac{2}{r} - \frac{1}{a} \right)}, \qquad a = \frac{r_{a} + r_{p}}{2}$$

so that lowering perigee from a circular orbit costs the difference between the circular speed and the speed
on the transfer at the same radius,

$$\Delta v = \sqrt{\frac{\mu}{r_{a}}} - \sqrt{\mu \left( \frac{2}{r_{a}} - \frac{1}{a} \right)} = 132.8 \text{ metres per second},$$

which is the work of the deorbit propulsion stage that GenCorp Aerojet was developing for V-201 \[[NASA account of the X-38 prototype crew return vehicle][ref_nasa_x38]\].

The deceleration that follows is what the crew feels, and for a shallow entry it is nearly independent of
the vehicle. The [Allen and Eggers][ref_allen_eggers] result for a ballistic entry gives the peak as

$$a_{\text{peak}} = \frac{v_{e}^{2} \sin \gamma_{e}}{2 e H}$$

where $H$ is the density scale height and $\gamma_{e}$ the entry flight path angle,
**and the ballistic coefficient does not appear at all.** At an entry speed of 7,800 metres per second and a
path angle of 1.4 degrees this is **3.87 g**. Lift relieves part of it, by the factor by which the vertical
force balance is eased,

$$k = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + (L/D)^{2}}}, \qquad a_{\text{lifting}} = k \, a_{\text{peak}}$$

which at a lift-to-drag ratio of 0.8 is **0.7809**.

- [Stern and Chu 1963][research_stern_chu_1963]
- [Shen 1963][research_shen_1963]
- [Kumagai 1964][research_kumagai_1964]
- [Vicente 1964][research_vicente_1964]
- [Lykoudis 1964][research_lykoudis_1964]
- [Lessing et al 1964][research_lessing_tunnell_1964]

| Lift-to-drag ratio | Relief factor | Peak with relief |
|---|---|---|
| 0.4 | 0.9285 | 3.60 g |
| 0.8 | 0.7809 | 3.02 g |
| 1.2 | 0.6402 | 2.48 g |

**A deconditioned or injured crew is why this matters.** The vehicle exists to bring down somebody who may
be ill, and a body that has spent months in free fall has lost plasma volume, bone and cardiac conditioning
through the well documented [effects of spaceflight on the human body][ref_deconditioning].
**The entry load is a medical constraint rather than a structural one**, which is a sentence that could not
be written about any other vehicle in this series.

### Hypersonic and Low-Speed Aerodynamics of the Shape

The [X-24A][ref_x24a] shape was flown twenty-eight times \[[Martin Marietta X-24A][ref_x24a]\] and
characterised across the speed range, which is
the reason it was chosen. **The X-38 therefore inherited an aerodynamic database rather than building one**,
and that is the single largest saving the borrowed shape produced.

### Guidance, Navigation and the Autonomous Descent

The vehicle flies itself throughout. **What is unusual is not the autonomy but where it ends.** A runway
lander must fly an autonomous approach with metre-level precision to a fixed strip. A parafoil lander must
only reach a chosen field and then flare, and the guidance problem is the one the
[guided airdrop][ref_airdrop] community had already been solving for cargo.

### Structure, Scale and a Shape That Grew

The X-38 inherited the X-24A outer mould line and enlarged it. Measuring the exponent that relates the two,
for a quantity $Q$ and a length $\ell$,

$$Q \propto \ell^{\,n} \qquad \Longrightarrow \qquad n = \frac{\ln \left( Q_{1} / Q_{2} \right)}{\ln \left( \ell_{1} / \ell_{2} \right)}$$

| Quantity | X-24A | X-38 Crew Return Vehicle | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| Length | 7.315 metres | 9.14 metres | 1.249 |
| Launch mass | 5,443 kilograms | 11,340 kilograms | 2.083 |

**The inputs are sourced as follows.** The X-24A is approximately 24 feet long, 7.315 metres
\[[Pilot safety for the X-24A lifting body vehicle][ref_cochrane_graham_1971]\]. The programme's history
gives approximately 12,000 pounds at launch, 5,443 kilograms \[[Wingless flight][book_rdalereed_1997]\],
and that is the launch mass in the table. No source consulted states the launch weight more precisely. The
Crew Return Vehicle is 30 feet long with a gross weight of 25,000 pounds, which are 9.14 metres and
11,340 kilograms \[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\].

**Mass scales as length to the 3.295 against 3 for geometric similarity.** A geometrically similar X-24A
grown to 9.14 metres would mass **10,618 kilograms**, and the X-38 masses 11,340, **a factor of 1.068**,

$$n = \frac{\ln (11{,}340 / 5{,}443)}{\ln (9.14 / 7.315)} = \frac{\ln 2.0834}{\ln 1.2495} = 3.295, \qquad
m_{\text{similar}} = 5{,}443 \times 1.2495^{3} = 10{,}618 \ \text{kg}, \qquad \frac{11{,}340}{10{,}618} = 1.068.$$

**The exponent stays above 3 across the uncertainty in both inputs.** The vehicle's builder gives
approximately 5,500 pounds empty with tankage for approximately 5,500 pounds of propellants and gases
\[[Pilot safety for the X-24A lifting body vehicle][ref_cochrane_graham_1971]\], about 11,000 pounds or
4,990 kilograms. A NASA study of lifting-body lift and drag tabulates the X-24A as 24.50 feet long, 7.468
metres \[[In-Flight Subsonic Lift and Drag Characteristics Unique to Blunt-Based Lifting Reentry Vehicles][research_saltzmanedwinj_wangkcharles_2007]\].
Taking each in turn,

$$n_{11{,}000\ \text{lb}} = \frac{\ln (11{,}340 / 4{,}990)}{\ln 1.2495} = 3.69, \qquad
n_{24.5\ \text{ft}} = \frac{\ln 2.0834}{\ln (9.14 / 7.468)} = 3.63,$$

and both exceed 3. **The margin over geometric similarity is therefore real but modest**, and its size
rests on approximate figures for a vehicle whose launch weight no source gives exactly.

**The shape was inherited and the mission was not.** The X-24A carried one pilot and a rocket engine. The
X-38 carries seven people, life support, a thermal protection system and a deorbit stage, and the mass grew
faster than the geometry because the payload grew faster than the airframe.

**This is the opposite sign from the previous article and the pair is worth keeping.** The
[X-37][related_post_a334_boeing_x37] shrank from the Shuttle orbiter with mass falling as length to the
**1.924**, more slowly than volume, because a small reusable vehicle keeps its fixed overhead. The X-38 grew
from the X-24A with mass rising as length to the **3.295**, faster than volume, because the mission grew
faster than the machine.
**Neither pair is geometrically similar and the reasons are different, which is a warning against reading a scaling exponent as a property of the technology.**

**The lifting-body line this vehicle belongs to is where the comparison has to be drawn**, since no two
members of it were built for the same job.

## The Flight Test Record

### What Actually Flew

**Three atmospheric vehicles flew and the orbital vehicle never did.**

| Vehicle | What it was | Fate |
|---|---|---|
| V-131 | Full aerodynamic scale of the X-24A shape | Drop tests from a B-52 |
| V-132 | As V-131, later configuration | Drop tests, including the highest and fastest |
| V-131R | Rebuilt at 80 percent of the Crew Return Vehicle | Drop tests with the full-size parafoil |
| V-201 | The orbital vehicle | **90 percent complete, never flown** |

The drop tests released the vehicle from the wing pylon of a [B-52][ref_b52] at altitudes between 7,600 and
13,700 metres, being 25,000 and 45,000 feet, over the lakebed at what is now the
[Armstrong Flight Research Center][ref_dryden] \[[NASA account of the X-38 prototype crew return vehicle][ref_nasa_x38]\].
In March 2000 V-132 was released at 11,900 metres, being 39,000 feet, and exceeded 800 kilometres per hour,
being 500 miles per hour, before deploying its parachutes. The Dryden news release gives its free flight as
44 seconds and the centre's photograph caption as 45
\[[NASA's X-38 crew rescue craft tests road to Earth][ref_dryden_release_2000]\]
\[[X-38 Arrival at NASA Dryden on June 4, 1997][ref_dvids_x38_arrival]\]. V-201 was 90 percent complete
when the project's cancellation was announced on 29 April 2002 \[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\].
**The vehicles were dropped, they flew a short unpowered profile, and then the decelerator system took over.**

### What the Drop Tests Could and Could Not Show

The atmospheric vehicles are **81.7 percent** of the orbital vehicle by length, being 24.5 feet long
\[[NASA account of the X-38 prototype crew return vehicle][ref_nasa_x38]\] against 30,

$$\frac{\ell_{\text{atmospheric}}}{\ell_{\text{orbital}}} = \frac{24.5 \ \text{ft}}{30 \ \text{ft}} = 0.817,$$

and they flew at between 15,000 and 24,000 pounds, which are 6,804 and 10,886 kilograms, against its 11,340
\[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\]. The 24,000 pound maximum is the encyclopaedia's figure. The NASA account gives
only that vehicle weights range from 15,000 pounds to about 25,000 pounds, in a passage that introduces the
orbital V-201 immediately after, so it does not separate the drop-test vehicles from the orbital one
\[[NASA account of the X-38 prototype crew return vehicle][ref_nasa_x38]\].
**Under the same canopy that is a very small difference in the quantity that matters.**

| Mass under the full canopy | Sink rate |
|---|---|
| 6,804 kilograms | 4.31 metres per second |
| 10,886 kilograms | 5.45 metres per second |
| 11,340 kilograms, the orbital vehicle | 5.56 metres per second |

**The heaviest drop test came within a factor of 1.021 of the orbital vehicle's sink rate**, and the reason
it came so close is a square root. At fixed canopy area and fixed coefficients the glide relation leaves
only the weight under the radical, so

$$V_{\text{vertical}} \propto \sqrt{m} \qquad \Longrightarrow \qquad \frac{\Delta V}{V} \approx \frac{1}{2} \frac{\Delta m}{m}.$$

**A mass shortfall of 4.00 percent is therefore a sink shortfall of about 2 percent**, and the landing
system was tested at very nearly its design condition. **The square root is doing the work**, since the same
shortfall in a quantity that scaled linearly would have been twice as far from condition.

- [Horinouchi et al 1964][research_horinouchi_hosoda_1964]

**The entry was never tested at all.** No X-38 ever went to orbit, no X-38 ever met the atmosphere at
orbital speed, and no X-38 thermal protection system was ever exposed to entry heating in flight.
**The programme proved the half of the problem that could be proved from a B-52 and left the other half entirely unproven**,
which is a precise statement of what was and was not demonstrated.

### The Cancellation

The programme was cancelled on 29 April 2002 without producing a flown orbital vehicle. The space agency's
account gives the total projected cost to develop and flight test the X-38s as approximately 700 million dollars,
against original estimates of more than two billion dollars for a capsule-type Crew Return Vehicle \[[NASA account of the X-38 prototype crew return vehicle][ref_nasa_x38]\].
**The stated reason was the station's own cost growth**, which led to a reduced configuration without the American
Crew Return Vehicle, whose crew of three could be served by the Soyuz already flying \[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\].

**The lifeboat was cancelled because the station shrank to fit the lifeboat it already had.** The seven-seat
requirement disappeared, and with it the only reason to build a seven-seat vehicle.

## Comparison With Ground Prediction

**The record holds one true comparison between a ground prediction and the flown system, and it is a disagreement.**
A vehicle-level load model, worked through in the section headed Sizing From First Principles, says that a
canopy of 696.8 square metres could have been inflated in a single step inside three g at a deployment speed
near twenty-seven metres per second. The flown system used five [reefed][ref_reefing] stages. The section
headed Where the Framing Breaks Down explains why the conclusion drawn from that disagreement is an inference
from a failure, and why its margin is only twenty percent in deployment speed. No title in the survey
reports this canopy's inflation loads against which to set the flown ones.

**The aerodynamic prediction was inherited rather than made.** The [X-24A][ref_x24a] shape had flown
twenty-eight times between 1969 and 1971, and the programme took its aerodynamic database instead of running
the wind-tunnel campaign that a new lifting body would have needed. The drop tests therefore flew a shape
whose behaviour was already known from flight. The article reports no comparison between the drop-test
aerodynamics and that database, and the record assembled here does not supply one.

**The parafoil figures in this article are predictions with no flight value set beside them here.** The
sink rate of 5.56 metres per second, the airspeed of 17.58 metres per second and the energy ratio of 26.2
rest on a lift coefficient of 0.80 and a glide ratio of 3, which are typical values assumed for a large ram-air
canopy. Aerodynamics from large-scale parafoil test programmes, one of them the X-38's own, are reported in
\[[Iacomini and Cerimele 1999][research_iacomini_cerimele_1999_b]\]
\[[Iacomini and Cerimele 1999][research_iacomini_cerimele_1999]\] and
\[[Madsen et al 2003][research_madsen_sostaric_2003]\], and the article does not take its coefficients from them. The table in the section headed The Flight Test Record that sets each drop-test mass beside
a sink rate is computed from the same relation and is not a measured result.
**What flight established is the condition of the test and not the value of the coefficients.** The heaviest
drop test came within a factor of 1.021 of the orbital vehicle's computed sink rate, so the landing system
flew at very nearly its design condition.

**The entry predictions have no flight counterpart at all.** The deorbit increment of 132.8 metres per
second, the ballistic peak of 3.87 g and the relieved peak of 3.02 g at a lift-to-drag ratio of 0.8 are
calculations. No X-38 reached orbit, and the orbital vehicle V-201 was 90 percent complete when the programme
was cancelled on 29 April 2002. **For the half of the problem the vehicle was built to solve, flight returned
nothing to compare**, which the section headed Epistemic State records as the question the record does not
settle.

## What the Data Changed

### What the Programme Settled

**That a very large ram-air canopy can be flown under control.** A canopy of seven hundred square metres was the
largest parafoil ever made \[[NASA X-38][ref_x38]\], and the programme flew it repeatedly, with staged reefing, autonomous guidance and
a flare.
That is a decelerator result and not a spacecraft result, and it stands on its own.

**That the guided-parafoil approach scales to a crewed vehicle's mass.** The heaviest drop test was within
two percent of the orbital vehicle's sink rate, so the landing system was demonstrated at condition.

### What It Fed Into

**The guided airdrop field took the work and kept going.** Large steerable canopies for cargo delivery
entered military service through the Joint Precision Airdrop System
\[[Benney et al 2005][research_benney_barber_2005]\] \[[Joshua and Eaton 2013][research_joshua_eaton_2013]\],
among them a 4,500 kilogram class guided system \[[George et al 2005][research_george_carter_2005]\], and the
control and wind-estimation problems the X-38 solved for a crewed vehicle are the problems that field works on, as
the cluster on guidance, homing and flare control of parafoils below shows.

**The lifting body line continued without the parafoil.** A commercial lifting-body spaceplane, Sierra Space's
Dream Chaser, is being developed to launch vertically and land on a conventional runway, which is the answer the
X-38 declined to give. Its first flight, a free-flight demonstration that no longer visits the station, is
scheduled no earlier than late 2026 \[[Dream Chaser][ref_dream_chaser]\].

### What It Failed to Influence

**No crewed vehicle in the record assembled here has since landed under a parafoil.** The crewed capsules in
the survey descend under round parachutes, the Soyuz under a braking parachute and then a main parachute
\[[Soyuz spacecraft][ref_soyuz]\], and Shenzhou and Orion under parachute systems of the same kind
\[[Jia et al 2014][research_jia_wang_2014]\] \[[Lichodziejewski et al 2009][research_lichodziejewski_taylor_2009]\].
**The X-38's specific answer, the gliding canopy for a crewed return, has no flown successor.**

### Guided Parafoils Became an Industry While the Spacecraft Did Not

**The single largest change since the X-38 is that autonomous guided parafoil delivery became operational and ordinary.**
The problems the programme solved for one crewed vehicle became routine for cargo, and the modern literature
on wind estimation, homing guidance, flare timing and touchdown accuracy is deep, active and largely open.

### Parafoil Aerodynamics and Flight Dynamics

**The apparent-mass terms that dominate a light canopy's dynamics and the fluid-structure coupling that sets its shape are now computed rather than measured**,
which is the largest methodological change in the field since the programme ran.

### Inflation, Reefing and Opening Loads

**The question this article could not answer from a vehicle-level model is exactly the question this literature answers.**
Modern inflation analysis couples the canopy structure to the flow and predicts the load distribution across
the suspension lines, which is where the stage count actually comes from.

### Human Tolerance, Deconditioning and the Medical Constraint

**The entry load limit for this vehicle is a medical number and the medical literature has moved.** What a
deconditioned crew tolerates after months in free fall is better characterised now than it was, and it bears
directly on the entry profile a lifeboat should fly.

### Parachute aerodynamics, stability and descent

**Round and cross canopies in steady descent.** Parachute drag, wake, stability, cluster behaviour and descent dynamics. Parachute titles that name no inflation, recovery application, supersonic or planetary use, textile or gliding canopy are gathered here.

### Parachute recovery of spacecraft, boosters and aircraft

**Round-canopy recovery of particular vehicles.** The Apollo, Gemini, Orion and sample-return parachute systems, booster and stage recovery, and recovery systems for aircraft and unmanned aircraft.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

### The Parafoil Numbers Rest on Two Assumed Coefficients

**The lift coefficient of 0.80 and the glide ratio of 3 are typical values, assumed rather than taken from the flight results of the X-38 parafoil test programme** \[[Madsen et al 2003][research_madsen_sostaric_2003]\].
The sizing section computes the sensitivity instead of asserting robustness, and the sink rate moves from
5.56 to 6.42 metres per second at a coefficient of 0.60 and to 4.97 at 1.00.
**The energy ratio moves only as the first power**, between 19.7 and 32.8 across the same range, so the
conclusion survives and the individual sink rates should not be quoted to three figures.

### The Runway Comparison Uses a Speed the X-38 Never Had

**Ninety metres per second is a lifting body's touchdown speed and it is taken from the X-24 family rather than from the X-38**,
which never landed horizontally and had no landing gear. The comparison is therefore between this vehicle
under its canopy and the vehicle it would have had to be if it landed on a runway.
**That is the correct comparison for the argument and it is not a measurement of two things that both existed.**

### The Landing Opportunity Table Is an Order of Magnitude and Nothing Finer

**The site counts are illustrative.** Nobody has enumerated the world's flat unobstructed areas, the
reachability model treats sites as uniformly distributed over the covered band, and real site selection
involves weather, overflight permission, recovery logistics and terrain.
**What survives is the direction and the scale, which is a factor of thousands and not a factor of two.**

### The Reefing Conclusion Is an Inference From a Failure, and Its Margin Is Narrow

**The article concludes that the canopy's own structure sets the stage count, and it concludes that because a vehicle-level load model fails to explain the flown system.**
That is an argument from the absence of an alternative and not a measurement of canopy loads, and a
published analysis of this canopy's line loads during inflation would settle it. **The survey holds titles on
this canopy's deployment and on its aerodynamics at its opening stages** \[[Ranier 2001][research_ranierchris_2001]\]
\[[Haubelt et al 2001][research_haubelt_melton_2001]\] \[[Neville][research_neville]\], and none of their titles
names the line loads.

**The margin is also narrower than the three g figure suggests.** The full canopy reaches three g at 32.28
metres per second, which is only **1.20 times** the assumed deployment speed, and it reaches two g at 26.36,
which is **below** it.
**A deployment twenty percent faster than assumed, or a limit set at two g rather than three, would put the steady load back into contention**,
and the deployment speed itself is taken from a loosely reported figure.

### The Scaling Comparison Puts Two Unlike Machines Side by Side

The X-24A was a piloted rocket research aircraft and the X-38 was a lifeboat designed for seven crew whose test vehicles flew under autonomous guidance.
**Measuring an exponent between them is legitimate as a description and misleading as an explanation**, and
the article says which it means.

**The exponent is also sensitive to figures that are easy to get wrong.** It rests on two published numbers
for a vehicle retired in 1971, and moving the X-24A length by half a metre or its mass by three hundred
kilograms moves the exponent by several tenths. **What is robust is the sign**, that mass grew faster than
the cube of length, and that is the only thing the argument uses.

### Nothing Here Is Validated Against an Orbital Flight

**No X-38 ever flew in space.** Every entry number in this article is a calculation with no flight data
behind it, and the vehicle that would have produced that data sits in storage.
**An article about a vehicle that never flew its mission cannot verify the half of its argument that concerns the mission.**

## The Source Base

### The Keystone Literature Is Not a Spacecraft Literature

**The papers this article rests on are about canopies and most of them mention no vehicle at all.** Of the
3,543 research records, 1,841 carry a decelerator term in their title, being parachute, parafoil,
paraglider, parawing, decelerator, drogue, ballute, ram-air, canopy, reefing, airdrop or aerial delivery, and
1,772 of those name no spacecraft, entry, station, lifting body or crew return vehicle. A source base
built from spacecraft vocabulary would have missed nearly the whole of it, which is the reason the selection here
anchors on decelerator terms as heavily as on the programme.

### Both Halves, Counted and Not Only Divided

| Half | Count | Share of dated |
|---|---|---|
| The period and all that precedes it, through 2006 | 1,760 | 51.5 percent |
| Contemporary, 2007 onward | 1,659 | 48.5 percent |
| Undated | 124 | |
| **Total research records** | **3,543** | |

**The period count is 1,760 and the contemporary count is 1,659.** Both are given because a
contemporary survey lowers the period **share** while leaving the period **count** untouched, so the share alone
would understate the period literature. **The median publication year is 2006.** Every record of both halves is listed by subject in the cluster rows under The Contemporary
Literature.

**A date is not a measure of primacy and this article reports both.** The technical report servers hold
original research reports almost exclusively, while a journal index holds originals, reviews and comment
together, so the report share is the closest available proxy for how much of the base is primary.

| Measure | Count | Share |
|---|---|---|
| Technical reports, all eras | 1,091 | 30.8 percent |
| Technical reports through 2006 | 778 | 22.0 percent |
| Journal and conference records | 2,452 | 69.2 percent |

A further **102** reference works are listed below, and they carry the relations that no journal article
states because every textbook does.
The count is the number of entries in that list.

**The research survey holds 3,543 research records drawn from Crossref, the NASA Technical Reports Server and
the Defense Technical Information Center**, of which 1,091 are report primaries, being
30.8 percent, by the identifier rule set out under The Definition of Primary.

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report
primary when its address is on the NASA Technical Reports Server or it carries a digital object identifier under
the 10.21236 prefix, which Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports. Primary sources in
substance are the space agency and Army technical reports on decelerator systems, the programme's own reports from
1995 to 2002, and the journal papers contemporary with each relation used.

**The report literature matters for this subject.** Of the 631 decelerator titles dated 2000 or earlier,
262 are report primaries by the identifier rule, being 41.5 percent, against
30.8 percent of the survey as a whole. **The rule does not reach the journal and conference papers
among the original sources**, so 30.8 percent is a lower bound on how much of the survey is
first-hand. **A date and a primacy are different measurements and both are reported above**, since a cutoff
separates period from contemporary and says nothing about whether a record is an original report or a commentary
on one.

### Thin Subjects

**The subjects an argument rests on are not the subjects its vocabulary names.** This article displays relations
drawn from the Allen and Eggers entry peak, the opening-shock factor, the square-cube law and the drogue sizing
condition, and the literature those relations rest on is filed mostly under other words.

**Eight subjects that this article's derivations depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below
is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a
hyphen read as a space. Where a row joins two groups of terms with the word together, a title must carry a term
from each group.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| the lift coefficient and glide ratio of a ram-air canopy | parafoil, parafoils, ram air, paraglider, paragliders, parawing, parawings, gliding parachute, gliding parachutes together with lift coefficient, lift coefficients, glide ratio, lift to drag, lift and drag, lift drag | 4 |
| the flare of a parafoil | parafoil, parafoils, ram air, paraglider, paragliders, parawing, parawings, gliding parachute, gliding parachutes together with flare, flaring, flared | 3 |
| reefing of a ram-air canopy | parafoil, parafoils, ram air, paraglider, paragliders, parawing, parawings, gliding parachute, gliding parachutes together with reefing, reefed, slider, sliders, disreefing | 7 |
| the opening-shock factor | opening shock factor, opening shock factors, opening load factor, opening force coefficient, opening force coefficients, inflation force coefficients, opening factor | 8 |
| the entry peak deceleration | peak deceleration, maximum deceleration, deceleration loads, drag deceleration, Allen Eggers | 3 |
| crossrange | crossrange, cross range | 2 |
| landing opportunities for a returning spacecraft | landing opportunities, landing opportunity, deorbit opportunities, calldown, landing site coverage, landing site availability | 3 |
| the square-cube law and mass-length scaling | square cube | 3 |

**The canopy lift coefficient is the article's most load-bearing assumption, and its heading is thin while its
subject is not.** Four titles name a lift
coefficient, a glide ratio or lift and drag for a gliding canopy, while the ram-air cluster holds
288 records, of which
35 are titled as aerodynamic characteristics, wind tunnel tests or flight tests. Those titles are where a measured coefficient would be reported, and a count of the heading
alone could not have said so.

**The flare is thin and the article's flare result does not need it**, since the energy identity derived above is
exact within the steady-glide assumption, and three titles name the flare of
a parafoil or parawing.

**Reefing a ram-air canopy is mostly a slider literature.** Of the seven
titles, five name a slider, the device that reefs a sport or cargo canopy, and the staged
inflation of the X-38's own canopy is reported under the words opening stages and stages of inflation in the cluster
on the X-38 and crew return vehicles.

**The opening-shock factor is settled engineering that is recomputed for each system.** Most of the
eight titles that name it treat particular canopies, among them the Orion
and Ares parachutes and ram-air airdrop canopies.

**Some knowledge is settled and has stopped generating papers.** The entry peak deceleration relation dates from
1958 and appears in every entry textbook, so its three records are not a
gap.

**Crossrange is almost absent as a title word.** Two titles name it, and the lifting-entry
literature reaches the subject through footprints and range control instead, with five
titles naming a footprint and two naming range control.

**The landing-opportunity argument rests on three
titles**, among them a report on Crew Return Vehicle deorbit opportunities, and the site counts in the article are
illustrative for that reason.

**The square-cube law has no spacecraft literature here.** Its three
titles concern airships, the aircraft industry and mechanical systems, so the exponent measured above rests on the
two vehicles' published dimensions alone.

**And the last one is the article.** Of the 66 titles that name the X-38, 62 date from 2006
or earlier and 2 from 2007 or later. **The programme stopped in 2002 and the literature stopped with
it.**

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject.** The subject is
the decelerator family, the vehicle and its lifting-body line, atmospheric entry and its protection, deorbit and
the choice of landing place, the occupant's tolerance of acceleration and of spaceflight deconditioning, landing
impact attenuation, crew escape and spaceflight risk, the station the vehicle served, and the sizing of entry
vehicles. Many words this subject depends on also name something else, and a record that shares only such a word
is excluded. Parachute also names a heart valve, a surgical flap, a medical device, a pre-school game, a creek
with an oil shale plant beside it, the children sent abroad to study alone and an executive's severance, and
reentry also names a prisoner's return to the community and a cardiac arrhythmia. Drogue is also the French word for
a drug and an ocean current marker, and a drogue in aerial refuelling is a different device. Reefing also names a
surgical technique and reef a geological formation, opening force also names the force of a jaw, a spun fibre or a
switch, and airdrop also names a distribution of cryptocurrency and a wireless file-sharing device.

**Records that apply this subject's constructs to a distant domain are excluded too.** Clinical orthostatic
intolerance in patient populations and its treatment, with no spaceflight or ground-analogue context, is excluded,
and so are satellite and debris deorbit devices and uncontrolled reentry, missile tracking and reentry vehicle
hardness, station subsystems, payloads and science, sports landing biomechanics, the crashworthiness of road and
rail vehicles, and risk assessment of nuclear plants and other non-space systems. Correction, erratum,
clarification and withdrawal notices, peer-review reports, reviews of books, news items, encyclopedia, dictionary and glossary entries that are unsigned or of two pages or
fewer, patent abstracts and front matter are excluded as well, because a survey counts research works and those are
parts of works or editorial events rather than works. A signed encyclopedia article and a textbook chapter are
research works and stay. Twin deposits of the same document are counted once.

**Some records of doubtful relevance are kept.** Crushable and energy-absorbing structures that name no vehicle,
acceleration tolerance studies in animals, the aerodynamics and heating of ballistic missile reentry vehicles,
Mars and lunar entry, descent and landing, aircraft ejection seats, the Apollo-Soyuz mission reports and plasma
blackout during entry each share the physics, the structure or the occupant with this subject, so each is kept and
a reader may weigh it accordingly. **The two broadest clusters have stated rules.** Parachute aerodynamics,
stability and descent holds the parachute titles that name no inflation, recovery application, supersonic or
planetary use, textile or gliding canopy, and entry vehicle aerodynamics, aerothermodynamics and design holds the
entry titles that name no trajectory, guidance or thermal protection.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading
judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about
titles only.

### What the Record Does Not Contain

**No orbital flight data of any kind.** No entry heating measurement, no orbital thermal protection
performance, no deorbit accuracy, no orbital parafoil deployment. The vehicle that would have produced all
of it was ninety percent complete when the programme stopped.

## Epistemic State

### Historical Fact

The programme began at the Johnson Space Center in 1995 to demonstrate technology for a Crew Return Vehicle
for the International Space Station. The aerodynamic shape was taken from the Martin Marietta X-24A. Scaled
Composites built the atmospheric test airframes, and the European Space Agency and the German Aerospace Center took part
in the programme. Three atmospheric vehicles flew, being V-131, V-132 and V-131R, dropped from a B-52 at
altitudes up to about 13,700 metres. In March 2000 V-132 flew freely for 44 or 45 seconds, by the two NASA
accounts, after release at 11,900 metres and exceeded 800 kilometres per hour. The descent system used a 7,500 square foot ram-air parafoil,
the largest ever made, deployed in five reefed stages. The orbital vehicle V-201 reached about 90
percent completion and never flew. The vehicle was designed for seven crew. The programme was cancelled on
29 April 2002.

### Engineering Analysis

Every quantitative result here is computed from published dimensions and masses using standard relations.
**Three sensitivities are computed and none is asserted**, being the lift coefficient through the sizing,
the fixed mass fraction through the crew argument, and the deployment speed through the reefing argument.
**The first two leave their conclusions intact and the third narrows one.** The glide speeds, the energy
ratio, the wing loading, the area inversion, the deployment loads, the entry deceleration, the deorbit
increment and the scaling exponents all follow from published figures.
**The identity that the flare's available energy exceeds what it must remove by the square of the glide ratio is exact within the steady-glide assumption**
and carries no vehicle property at all.

### Inference

That the canopy's own structure rather than crew tolerance sets the reefing stage count is an inference from
a vehicle-level load model failing to explain the flown system by a wide margin. That abandoning the runway
buys availability rather than costing capability is an inference from an order-of-magnitude
landing-opportunity argument. That the borrowed X-24A shape was the programme's largest single saving is an
inference from the absence of any X-38 aerodynamic development campaign in the record.

### What the Record Does Not Settle

**Whether the vehicle would have worked.** No X-38 flew in space, no entry was ever demonstrated, no thermal
protection system was exposed to orbital entry heating, and the parafoil was never deployed after an entry.
**The landing system was tested at nearly its design condition and the mission was not tested at all**, and
no amount of calculation closes that gap.

## Out of Scope

The Soyuz as a lifeboat and its own return system, which deserves separate treatment. The station's assembly
sequence and the crew-size decisions that drove the requirement. The detailed design of the deorbit
propulsion module. The European crew transport studies that shared the programme's structure work. The
commercial crew vehicles that eventually solved the problem differently. Guided airdrop for cargo, which is
surveyed here and not analysed.

## Conclusion

**The X-38 asked what a runway costs and answered it in one ratio.** A lifting body arrives on a runway
carrying **26.2 times** the kinetic energy of the same mass under a ram-air canopy, and the ratio is the
square of the speed ratio with the mass cancelling out entirely. Everything the runway demands follows from
that energy, being the speed, the strip, the gear and the crossrange to reach one of the few places where
all three exist.

**Giving it up bought availability, which for a lifeboat is the whole requirement.** A vehicle needing a
certified runway gets a landing opportunity every ten days. A vehicle needing a flat field gets four hundred
a day.
**The crossrange that the earlier lifting-entry articles of this series treated as a figure of merit turns out to have been the price of a decision, and the X-38 is the vehicle that declined to pay it.**

**The second answer is that the record-breaking canopy was an output rather than an ambition.** Area varies
as the inverse square of the sink rate, so wanting one metre per second less costs half as much canopy
again, and seven seats aboard a station of seven fixed the mass.
**A single-seat version would still have needed 58.6 percent of the same canopy**, because the airframe and
the heat shield do not shrink with the crew.

**Two smaller results are worth carrying because neither needed an assumption.** The flare's available
energy exceeds what it must remove by the square of the glide ratio, **exactly**, with no mass, area or
density appearing, so a flare is never limited by energy. And the peak entry deceleration carries no
ballistic coefficient at all, which makes the **3.87 g** a property of the trajectory rather than of the
vehicle and the entry load a medical constraint rather than a structural one.

**The third answer came from a calculation that failed.** A vehicle-level load model says the whole canopy
could have been inflated in one step inside three g, and the flown system used five stages.
**The constraint was never the crew.** It is the canopy's own structure, and reefing exists to make a
seven-hundred-square-metre inflation orderly rather than to make it gentle.
**The margin on that conclusion is only twenty percent in deployment speed**, which the article states and
does not leave to be assumed.

**What the programme did not answer is the half it was built for.** The landing system was demonstrated
within two percent of its design sink rate. **The entry was never demonstrated at all**, and the vehicle
that would have demonstrated it was ninety percent complete when the station it was meant to serve shrank to
a size its existing lifeboat could already carry.
**The X-38 was cancelled not because it failed but because the requirement went away**, and that is the
reason its own record is thin.
